<a id="pm-s1"></a>
## 1. Abstract and result contract

> We construct smooth forces for three-dimensional incompressible Navier–Stokes flow, from rest at any fixed positive computable viscosity, whose velocity field detects whether a prescribed machine halts. On the unit flat torus, a pointwise test of the third velocity component uses successively shorter stirring intervals in a fixed spatial region. On $`\mathbb R^2\times\mathbb T`$, an integral test uses an expanding array of translation regions and a force with globally bounded mixed derivatives. The vertical velocity solves an advection–diffusion equation: short bursts control its pointwise error in the first construction, and a moving cutoff controls the total escaped mass in the second.

- **C1** (construction, [source](#stmt-thm-main)): For each positive computable $\nu$, a machine and a finite input effectively determine each of the following two forces.* 1. *On $\mathbb T^3$, a smooth force has a unique global smooth solution in the torus class above, with pressure zero, for which <a href="#eq:torus-event" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:torus-event">[eq:torus-event]</a> holds exactly when the machine halts. The force is supported in $K\times\mathbb T$ for a compact $K\subset(0,1)^2$ independent of time. Its derivatives are bounded on every finite time interval; their bounds may grow with that interval.* 2. *On $\mathbb R^2\times\mathbb T$, a smooth force and all its mixed derivatives are globally bounded. On each finite time interval its horizontal support is compact. It has a unique global smooth solution in <a href="#eq:comparison-class" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:comparison-class">[eq:comparison-class]</a>, with pressure zero and integrable nonnegative third component. Event <a href="#eq:plane-event" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:plane-event">[eq:plane-event]</a> holds exactly when the machine halts.* *The sets and thresholds in both observations are independent of the machine and input. The compact set $K$ in the first construction may depend on that instance, but is independent of time. Both force prescriptions install all the required local instructions, without executing the selected computation.
  - Explanation: Two effective force constructions encode halting in fixed velocity observations from rest. The torus construction has finite-time derivative bounds and fixed spatial support; the expanding construction has globally bounded mixed derivatives and compact horizontal support on finite intervals.
- **C2** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-compact-detector)): For $V,p,E$ as above, choose either of the two pairs $$\label{eq:two-constants} (d,H)=(1/32,10^9)\quad\hbox{or}\quad(1/16,10^8).$$ There is a smooth force on $\mathbb T^3$, effectively determined by $V,p$, for which zero initial velocity has a global smooth solution, unique in the classical torus class of Section <a href="#sec:introduction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:introduction">1</a>, with pressure zero and the following properties.* 1. *If $\Psi_k(p)\in E$ for some integer $k\ge0$, then $u_3(t,Y,z)>1/2$ for some finite $t$ and some $Y\in E$.* 2. *If $\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}_{\mathbb T^2}(\Psi_s(p),E)\ge2d$ for every $s\ge0$, then $u_3(t,Y,z)<1/2$ for every $t\ge0$ and $Y\in E$.* *The third component is nonnegative. At viscosity one, its total horizontal mass is less than $10^{-11}$. The same conclusions and threshold hold for every positive computable viscosity, with the time and horizontal velocity rescaled as in <a href="#eq:compact-viscosity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:compact-viscosity">[eq:compact-viscosity]</a>. The force is supported in the union of the horizontal support of $V$ and one fixed small neighborhood of $p$, times $\mathbb T$. All smoothness and uniqueness assertions concern every finite time interval.
  - Explanation: Injection, short stirring, and heat-only waiting separate an integer-phase target visit from an orbit that stays uniformly away from the target. The latter hypothesis includes intermediate phases.
- **C3** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-torus-scalar)): For smooth $a,h$ on $[0,T]\times\mathbb T^2$ with $\mathop{\mathrm{div}}_Ya=0$, smooth initial data give a unique smooth solution of $w_t+a\cdot\nabla_Yw=\Delta_Yw+h$. Nonnegative initial data and source give $w\ge0$. Homogeneous evolution contracts the supremum norm. On an interval starting at $t_0$, zero initial data and $\|h\|\le A$ imply $\\|w(t)\\|_\infty\le A(t-t_0)$.
  - Explanation: Smooth divergence-free transport on the torus preserves positivity and supplies comparison bounds for scalar diffusion with a source.
- **C4** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-heat-bounds)): For either $(d,H)$ in <a href="#eq:two-constants" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:two-constants">[eq:two-constants]</a>, the unit two-torus heat kernel satisfies $$\label{eq:heat-bound} K(\tau,Z)\le H \quad\hbox{if }\tau\ge1 \quad\hbox{or}\quad\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}_{\mathbb T^2}(Z,0)\ge d.$$
  - Explanation: Explicit heat-kernel bounds control old scalar mass after waiting and fresh diffusive tails away from their transported support.
- **C5** (construction, [source](#stmt-cbe-scalar-lemma)): Equation <a href="#cbe:scalar" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cbe:scalar">[cbe:scalar]</a> has a global smooth solution. Every space-time derivative is continuous in time with values in $C_0(\mathbb R^2)\cap L^1(\mathbb R^2)$, where $C_0$ denotes continuous functions tending to zero at infinity. Furthermore, $$\label{cbe:mass} \rho\ge0,\qquad \mathcal M(t):=\int_{\mathbb R^2}\rho(t,X)\,dX=\theta(t)\le1.$$
  - Explanation: The noncompact scalar evolution is smooth with integrable, vanishing tails and nonnegative mass controlled by the prescribed loading profile.
- **C6** (theorem, [source](#stmt-auto-corollary-1)): There is no algorithm deciding either fixed velocity event in Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> for every force description produced by its respective construction.
  - Explanation: A decision procedure for either constructed velocity event would decide machine halting. The impossibility concerns the force descriptions supplied by the respective construction.

<a id="pm-s2"></a>
## 2. Definitions, validity and bounds

- **flat torus** ([source](#eq:NS)): Write $\mathbb T=\mathbb R/\mathbb Z$.
- **effective force prescription** ([source](#eq:NS)): An effective force prescription is a finite program evaluating $f$ and each requested mixed derivative, to any rational error, at computably supplied space-time arguments.
- **classical competitor** ([source](#eq:comparison-class)): A classical competitor has continuous velocity, time derivative, spatial derivatives through order two, pressure and pressure gradient on every finite closed time cylinder, and satisfies the equations pointwise.
- **horizontal residual and vertical source** ([source](#eq:triangular-force)): Let $Y$ range over $\mathbb T^2$ or $\mathbb R^2$. Suppose $a(t,Y)$ is divergence free, and prescribe the horizontal residual and the vertical source by $$\label{eq:triangular-force} F=\partial_ta+(a\cdot\nabla_Y)a-\nu\Delta_Ya, \qquad f(t,Y,z)=(F(t,Y),h(t,Y)).$$
- **injection profiles and scales** ([source](#eq:compact-scales)): For $n\ge1$, put $$\label{eq:compact-scales} \begin{aligned} b_n&=10^{-6}(2R)^{-n},&g_n(Y)&=g((Y-p)/b_n),\\ D_n&=2Cb_n^{-2}(1+nL)R^{3n},& \delta_n&=\frac1{100(1+D_n)},\qquad t_n=2n. \end{aligned}$$
- **eventually blank stack encoding** ([source](#cbe:stack)): The integer $\sum_{j\ge0}d_{\alpha_j}b^j$ encodes an eventually blank stack whose top is $\alpha_0$.
- **configuration address** ([source](#cbe:addresses)): At level $n$, give the triple $(i,x,y)$ the address $$\label{cbe:address} k=1+x+B_ny+B_n^2(i-1),\qquad 1\le k\le K_n.$$
- **noncompact scalar** ([source](#cbe:scalar)): Define $\rho$ by solving $$\label{cbe:scalar} \partial_t\rho+W\cdot\nabla_X\rho =\nu\Delta_X\rho+g,\qquad \rho(0,X)=0.$$
- **cutoff polynomial** ([source](#cbe:laplacian)): On $[0,1]$ put $P(s)=10s^3-15s^4+6s^5$, extending it by zero to the left and one to the right.
- **moving captured mass** ([source](#cbe:moving)): Take $R=R_0$ during loading and $R=R_n$ during the $n$th transition, and within each such interval put $$\mathcal I(t)=\int_{\mathbb R^2} \varphi_R(X-c(t))\rho(t,X)\,dX.$$

      "exact_definition": "Take $R=R_0$ during loading and $R=R_n$ during the $n$th transition, and within each such interval put $$\\mathcal I(t)=\\int_{\\mathbb R^2}\n            \\varphi_R(X-c(t))\\rho(t,X)\\,dX.$$",
      "interpretation": "The moving cutoff records scalar mass near the selected configuration.",
      "kind": "definition",
      "name": "moving captured mass",
      "scope": "Loading and transitions up to the first terminal endpoint."
    }
  ],
  "generation_status": "COMPLETE",
  "pm_id": "PM-376.7",
  "schema": "pm.objects.v2"
}

<a id="pm-s3"></a>
## 3. Proof roadmap

- **R1** [claim](#stmt-thm-main) / [proof](#sec:scalar-structure): Triangular scalar reduction and difference-energy uniqueness. -> A scalar solution supplies the velocity and uniqueness in the stated class.
- **R2** [claim](#stmt-lem-torus-scalar) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-torus-scalar): Fourier projection, energy estimates, diagonal convergence, and maximum comparison. -> Smooth scalar evolution with positivity and comparison bounds.
- **R3** [claim](#stmt-lem-heat-bounds) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-heat-bounds): Periodized Gaussian and geometric shell bounds. -> Explicit heat-kernel upper bounds.
- **R4** [claim](#stmt-thm-compact-detector) / [proof](#sec:compact-error): Flow derivative estimates and comparison against inviscid transport. -> Small active error and detection at integer-phase target entry.
- **R5** [claim](#stmt-thm-compact-detector) / [proof](#eq:wait-exclusion): Heat-kernel control of old mass and separated fresh support. -> No false target detection during stirring or waiting.
- **R6** [claim](#stmt-thm-main) / [proof](#sec:compact-application): Apply the detector to the supplied Hamiltonian processor. -> Fixed torus velocity observation detects halting.
- **R7** [claim](#stmt-thm-main) / [proof](#cbe:addresses): Integer stacks, reversible one-step lookup, and finite addresses. -> Distinct defined addresses have distinct next-level targets.
- **R8** [claim](#stmt-thm-main) / [proof](#cbe:gates): Separated translation gates with increasing radii and durations. -> Smooth horizontal transport with globally bounded mixed derivatives.
- **R9** [claim](#stmt-cbe-scalar-lemma) / [proof](#proof-stmt-cbe-scalar-lemma): Volterra contraction in smooth integrable-tail spaces, integration, and maximum comparison. -> Smooth nonnegative scalar with the required mass and tails.
- **R10** [claim](#stmt-thm-main) / [proof](#cbe:loss): Moving cutoff cancels transport and sums diffusion losses. -> Half-plane mass separates terminal and nonterminal runs.
- **R11** [claim](#stmt-thm-main) / [proof](#cbe:scalar-effective): Finite-time Picard truncation with derivative errors and certified spatial tails. -> Scalar derivatives and tails are effectively evaluable on finite intervals.
- **R12** [claim](#stmt-auto-corollary-1) / [proof](#proof-stmt-auto-corollary-1): Reduction from machine halting to either fixed velocity event. -> No decision algorithm exists for every constructed description.

<a id="pm-s4"></a>
## 4. Engineering Triptych

### Panel 1 - epistemic contract

- **I-stack**: The stack updates implement the indicated tape moves and stay within the next level bounds.
- **I-address**: Integer division recovers the configuration from its level address.
- **I-scalar**: The force prescription uses only the horizontal field and scalar source; the scalar is subsequently solved for.
- **I-loss**: The moving cutoff bounds escaped scalar mass through the first terminal endpoint.

### Panel 2 - negative knowledge

- **A-halting**: Decide eventual halting by stopping a finite simulation without a detection. -> Absence of detection on a finite interval does not decide the eventual event.
- **A-precision**: Replace the exact force prescription by finite-precision forcing and retain the detection theorem. -> The source supplies no stability guarantee for this replacement.
- **A-support**: Treat the bounded-force construction as uniformly confined in space or energy. -> Its expanding geometry does not supply these uniform bounds.
- **A-complexity**: Infer efficient execution from effective force evaluation. -> Effective computability gives no resource guarantee.

### Panel 3 - reference implementation and oracle

Applicability: `constructive`. Finite stack updates and address encoding are constructive arithmetic subresults. Their finite verification does not decide halting or certify the fluid solution.

```python
# INPUTS: JSON object with integer b and m, exhaustive finite stack domain
# PRECONDITIONS: b in {2,3}; m in {1,2}; all stacks below b**m
# OUTPUTS: JSON rows [move,state,x,y,beta,xnew,ynew,target_address]
# TIME: O(b**(2*m+1)*m) bit operations up to integer arithmetic costs
# SPACE: O(b**(2*m+1)*m) bits for output rows
# ARITHMETIC: exact python-flint fmpz integers
# FAILURE: malformed or unsupported input raises and exits nonzero
import json, sys
from flint import fmpz
cfg = json.loads(sys.stdin.read())
b, m = cfg['b'], cfg['m']
assert type(b) is int and type(m) is int and b in (2,3) and m in (1,2)
B = b**m
base = fmpz(b)
rows = []
for move in ('R','N','L'):
    for state in (1,2):
        for x in range(B):
            for y in range(B):
                for beta in range(b):
                    xx, yy, digit = fmpz(x), fmpz(y), fmpz(beta)
                    if move == 'R':
                        xn, yn = base*xx+digit, yy//base
                    elif move == 'N':
                        xn, yn = xx, yy-yy%base+digit
                    else:
                        xn, yn = xx//base, xx%base+base*digit+base*base*(yy//base)
                    target = 1+xn+(b*B)*yn+(b*B)**2*(state-1)
                    rows.append([move,state,x,y,beta,int(xn),int(yn),int(target)])
print(json.dumps(rows))
```

## 5. Domain engineering contracts

| domain | applies | strength | maturity | basis | why |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| software | True | moderate | adaptable | stated | The prescribed force has an effective evaluator for values and requested derivatives; this supports mathematical evaluation software under the stated computable-input contract. |
| games_simulation | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | A certified discrete fluid simulator or game mechanism is ABSENT. Exact velocity-event margins alone do not justify replacing the force by a finite-precision simulation. |
| tools | True | weak | adaptable | inferred | Finite-interval scalar evaluation supplies contraction, quadrature and tail-error requirements that can guide an evaluator with certified errors; a ready-made solver is ABSENT. |
| algorithms | True | strong | adaptable | stated | Finite address lookup and single-rule updates are explicitly computable, while deciding either eventual velocity event for the constructed forces is obstructed by the halting reduction. |
| data_structures | True | strong | adaptable | stated | Eventually blank tape halves are represented by positional integers, and integer division decodes each finite configuration address. |
| hardware_accelerators | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | A hardware implementation, acceleration guarantee and resource model are ABSENT. The smooth planar processor is a mathematical field, and the force evaluator has no complexity requirement. |
| crypto_security | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | A cryptographic primitive or security contract is ABSENT. Effective force evaluation and undecidable eventual observation do not establish a computational security guarantee. |
| machine_learning | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | A learning objective, training procedure and prediction guarantee are ABSENT. The source prescribes force evaluation directly from the machine and input. |
| optimisation_operations_research | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | An optimisation objective, feasibility solver and scheduling guarantee are ABSENT. The growing gate schedule is prescribed to control diffusion rather than proved optimal. |
| distributed_systems | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | A distributed protocol, communication model and fault guarantee are ABSENT. Simultaneously installed gates belong to a prescribed continuum field. |
| other (forced viscous-flow computation) | True | strong | adaptable | stated | Forced viscous-flow computation is the direct application: the scalar reduction and diffusion bounds connect prescribed smooth forcing to fixed velocity observations in the stated solution classes. |

<a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **L1** (hypothesis): The effective construction requires positive computable viscosity and starts from rest. Blocked use: ['Transferring the effective force construction to noncomputable viscosity without an oracle.', 'Applying the halting equivalence to arbitrary initial velocities.']
- **L2** (hypothesis): The force prescription evaluates requested mixed derivatives at computably supplied arguments with rational error and supplies the derivative bounds used in the construction. Blocked use: ['Replacing the effective derivative contract by pointwise force samples alone.']
- **L3** (formal_scope): Effective evaluability carries no computational complexity requirement. Blocked use: ['Inferring practical runtime or memory guarantees.']
- **L4** (formal_scope): Uniqueness is established for the explicitly constructed data in the stated comparison classes. Blocked use: ['Claiming existence for arbitrary three-dimensional data.', 'Extending uniqueness to competitors outside the stated regularity and pressure class.']
- **L5** (formal_scope): The torus force has finite-interval derivative bounds that can grow with the interval. Blocked use: ['Inferring uniform boundedness of the torus force for infinite time.']
- **L6** (formal_scope): Globally bounded force derivatives in the expanding construction do not assert a common compact support for all time or uniformly bounded total kinetic energy. Blocked use: ['Applying the expanding construction with a fixed compact horizontal support for all time.', 'Inferring uniformly bounded total kinetic energy.']
- **L7** (approximation): The strict observation margins concern the exact computable forces; force-perturbation stability and finite-precision replacement are not asserted. Blocked use: ['Transferring halting detection to a perturbed force or finite-precision force description without a separate stability proof.']
- **L8** (hypothesis): The compact detector requires nonhalting separation along the entire orbit, including transitions. Blocked use: ['Using integer-time separation alone to exclude false detection.']
- **L9** (edge_case): The compact detector force does not require an effective presentation of the open target; the target enters the orbit hypotheses. Blocked use: ['Treating the detector theorem as an algorithm that decides arbitrary target membership.']
- **L10** (hypothesis): Injectivity of stack transitions depends on the compiler incoming-rule properties, rather than on arbitrary machine tables. Blocked use: ['Applying disjoint target routing to a table without the incoming-rule properties.']
- **L11** (edge_case): An already halted original input is handled by a nonterminal preliminary compiler step. Blocked use: ['Omitting the preliminary step while asserting that the loading configuration lies on the nonterminal row.']
- **L12** (formal_scope): Compact horizontal force support on finite intervals does not make the scalar compactly supported; the comparison argument uses integrable tails. Blocked use: ['Truncating diffusion tails without an error bound.', 'Replacing integrable scalar tails by a compact-support assumption.']
- **L13** (formal_scope): The captured-mass argument is used through the first terminal endpoint to establish an existential velocity event. Blocked use: ['Inferring permanent mass capture or persistent detection after terminal arrival.']
- **L14** (approximation): Effective scalar evaluation on finite intervals must carry derivative error and integrable tail bounds across restarts. Blocked use: ['Restarting a scalar evaluator with only pointwise approximations.', 'Assuming the previous exact scalar has compact support.']
- **L15** (counterexample_scope): The undecidability conclusion concerns fixed velocity events for force descriptions produced by the respective constructions. Blocked use: ['Claiming a general-purpose decision procedure for the constructed events.', 'Treating lack of detection on a finite interval as proof of nonhalting.']

          "source_id": "PM-376.7"
        }
      ],
      "id": "L10",
      "kind": "hypothesis",
      "statement": "Injectivity of stack transitions depends on the compiler incoming-rule properties, rather than on arbitrary machine tables.",
      "support": "stated"

          "source_id": "PM-376.7"

      "kind": "edge_case",
      "statement": "An already halted original input is handled by a nonterminal preliminary compiler step.",
      "support": "stated"

        "Replacing integrable scalar tails by a compact-support assumption."
      ],

        }

<a id="pm-formal"></a>
### Formal statement (Lean 4)

Corpus status: formalized in the source corpus (toolchain `leanprover/lean4:v4.34.1`, Mathlib `d13f23b723b8a846827a245b89c10fc7d3f11612`). Not re-checked here; the statements give the exact quantifiers and hypotheses, not a proof.

Scope note of family 376 (`lean/docs/376.md`, verbatim):

> The following describes the scope of the Lean formalization related to the following accompanying paper(s):
>
> - [Geometric Programs for Solenoidal Forcing](../../preprints/Geometric-Programs-for-Solenoidal-Forcing-September-27-2026/manuscript.pdf)
> - [Computation under Rapidly Vanishing Navier–Stokes Forcing](../../preprints/Computation-under-Rapidly-Vanishing-Navier-Stokes-Forcing-September-27-2026/manuscript.pdf)
> - [Velocity-Field Detection of Computation in Forced Navier–Stokes Flows](../../preprints/Velocity-Field-Detection-of-Computation-in-Forced-Navier-Stokes-Flows-September-27-2026/manuscript.pdf)
> - [Incompressible Box Transport and Finite Computation](../../preprints/Incompressible-Box-Transport-and-Finite-Computation-September-27-2026/manuscript.pdf)
>
> ## Scope
>
> The formalization realizes finite families of prescribed positive diagonal maps on coding sheets by smooth incompressible flows on a flat three-torus. At every positive computable viscosity, it supplies a solution starting from rest with zero pressure and a force that is divergence free, has spatial mean zero, and is one-periodic from time zero. Each map acts on every point of its source sheet, and the construction provides effective bounds for all mixed derivatives.
>
> This selected statement covers the sheet programs. The paper's fixed-particle halting detector and its reciprocal maps on solid boxes are outside its scope.
>
> The formalization proves that a fluid particle can detect arbitrary finite-machine halting using alternating-coordinate memory in smooth incompressible Navier–Stokes flows whose velocity and force decay faster than every inverse power of time, including every mixed derivative. For each fixed positive computable viscosity, the construction starts from rest and provides effective forces, unique material trajectories, and an exact equivalence between entering a fixed open detector and halting.
>
> The velocity and force share one compact support for every machine and input. The solution-uniqueness assertion uses the stated energy comparison class.
>
> The formalization constructs smooth forced three-dimensional Navier–Stokes flows from rest whose velocity fields detect whether a prescribed machine halts, at any positive computable viscosity. On the unit flat torus, halting is equivalent to the vertical velocity exceeding $1/2$ somewhere in a fixed observation strip. On $\mathbb R^2\times\mathbb T$, it is equivalent to the integral of the nonnegative vertical velocity over the observation half-plane times the circle exceeding $1/2$.
>
> The torus force is confined to a fixed horizontal region. The cylindrical force has globally bounded mixed derivatives and compact horizontal support on every finite time interval. Both constructions have effective descriptions and uniqueness within their respective smooth solution-comparison classes.
>
> The formalization realizes finitely many positive diagonal affine maps of determinant one between rational solid boxes by a smooth compactly supported divergence-free flow. Source boxes are pairwise disjoint and target boxes are pairwise disjoint, while overlap between the two families is allowed. Each map holds on a neighborhood of the entire source box, and the velocity is supported in the middle half of the time interval.
>
> It also encodes every finite machine and input in a smooth, compactly supported incompressible Navier–Stokes flow on $\mathbb R^3$ starting from rest: the particle initially at $(4,0,0)$ enters the fixed open box $(-1,2)^3$ exactly when the machine halts. One velocity works for every viscosity $\nu>0$, with zero pressure and force $f_0+\nu f_1$. Velocity and force have bounded mixed derivatives and are one-periodic after time one; the repeated velocity depends only on the machine. The force is computable for computable $\nu$. Velocity is unique among smooth zero-data solutions with $u\in C_tH_x^2\cap C_t^1L_x^2$, bounded $u$ and $\nabla u$ on finite time intervals, and pressure modulo a function of time in $C_tH_x^1$.

structure SliceH2 (v : Space → Vect) : Prop where
  smooth : ∀ i, ContDiff ℝ 2 (fun x => v x i)
  per : ∀ i, periodic (fun x => v x i)
  square_integrable : ∀ i, MemLp (fun x => v x i) 2 measure
  derivative_square_integrable : ∀ i k, MemLp (D k (fun x => v x i)) 2 measure
  second_square_integrable : ∀ i k l, MemLp (D l (D k (fun x => v x i))) 2 measure
```

```lean
structure PressureH1 (q : Space → ℝ) : Prop where
  smooth : ContDiff ℝ 1 q
  per : periodic q
  square_integrable : MemLp q 2 measure
  derivative_square_integrable : ∀ k, MemLp (D k q) 2 measure
```

```

```lean
structure ComparisonClass (u : ℝ → Space → Vect) (p : ℝ → Space → ℝ) : Prop where
  velocity_H2 : ∀ T > 0, continuousH2On u T
  velocity_C1L2 : ∀ T > 0, C1L2On u T
  pressure_H1 : ∀ T > 0, continuousH1On p T
  time_derivative : ∀ t ≥ 0, ∀ x i,
    HasDerivWithinAt (fun s => u s x i) (dt u t x i) (Ici 0) t
  velocity_continuous : ∀ T > 0, ∀ i,
    ContinuousOn (fun y : ℝ × Space => u y.1 y.2 i) (Icc 0 T ×ˢ univ)
  time_continuous : ∀ T > 0, ∀ i,
    ContinuousOn (fun y : ℝ × Space => dt u y.1 y.2 i) (Icc 0 T ×ˢ univ)
  gradient_continuous : ∀ T > 0, ∀ i k,
    ContinuousOn (fun y : ℝ × Space => D k (fun x => u y.1 x i) y.2) (Icc 0 T ×ˢ univ)
  second_continuous : ∀ T > 0, ∀ i k l,
    ContinuousOn (fun y : ℝ × Space => D l (D k (fun x => u y.1 x i)) y.2) (Icc 0 T ×ˢ univ)
  pressure_continuous : ∀ T > 0,
    ContinuousOn (fun y : ℝ × Space => p y.1 y.2) (Icc 0 T ×ˢ univ)
  pressure_gradient_continuous : ∀ T > 0, ∀ i,
    ContinuousOn (fun y : ℝ × Space => D i (p y.1) y.2) (Icc 0 T ×ˢ univ)
  bounds : ∀ T > 0, ∃ B : ℝ, 0 ≤ B ∧
    (∀ t ∈ Icc 0 T, ∀ x i, |u t x i| ≤ B) ∧
    (∀ t ∈ Icc 0 T, ∀ x i k, |D k (fun y => u t y i) x| ≤ B)
```

  | (k,q)::ps => Polynomial.C (q:ℝ)*Polynomial.X^k + poly ps

```lean

  | [] => []
  | (k,q)::ps => (k+2,q)::(k+1,-q*k)::nextPoly ps

```lean
inductive Expr (n : ℕ) where
  | rat : ℚ → Expr n
  | var : Fin n → Expr n
  | add : Expr n → Expr n → Expr n
  | neg : Expr n → Expr n
  | mul : Expr n → Expr n → Expr n
  | inv : Expr n → Expr n
  | flat : Poly → Expr n → Expr n
  deriving Inhabited
```

```lean
noncomputable def eval (x : Fin n → ℝ) : Expr n → ℝ
  | .rat q => q
  | .var i => x i
  | .add a b => a.eval x + b.eval x
  | .neg a => -a.eval x
  | .mul a b => a.eval x * b.eval x
  | .inv a => (a.eval x)⁻¹
  | .flat p a => Effective.flat p (a.eval x)
```

```lean
def diff (i : Fin n) : Expr n → Expr n
  | .rat _ => .rat 0
  | .var j => .rat (if i=j then 1 else 0)
  | .add a b => .add (a.diff i) (b.diff i)
  | .neg a => .neg (a.diff i)
  | .mul a b => .add (.mul (a.diff i) b) (.mul a (b.diff i))
  | .inv a => .neg (.mul (a.diff i) (.mul (.inv a) (.inv a)))
  | .flat p a => .mul (.flat (nextPoly p) a) (a.diff i)
```

```lean
def jet : List (Fin n) → Expr n → Expr n
  | [], e => e
  | i::is, e => jet is (e.diff i)
```

```lean
structure ComparisonClass (u : VectorField 3) (p : ScalarField 3) : Prop where
  velocity_slices : ∀ t ≥ 0, ∀ i, ContDiff ℝ 2 (fun x => u t x i)
  pressure_slices : ∀ t ≥ 0, ContDiff ℝ 1 (p t)
  velocity_periodic : ∀ t ≥ 0, FactorsThrough (u t) PeriodicSpace.cover
  pressure_periodic : ∀ t ≥ 0, periodic (p t)
  pressure_mean : ∀ t ≥ 0, (∫ x, p t x ∂measure) = 0
  time_derivative : ∀ t ≥ 0, ∀ x i,
    HasDerivWithinAt (fun s => u s x i) (dt u t x i) (Ici 0) t
  velocity_continuous : ∀ T > 0, ∀ i,
    ContinuousOn (fun y : ℝ × Space => u y.1 y.2 i) (Icc 0 T ×ˢ univ)
  time_continuous : ∀ T > 0, ∀ i,
    ContinuousOn (fun y : ℝ × Space => dt u y.1 y.2 i) (Icc 0 T ×ˢ univ)
  gradient_continuous : ∀ T > 0, ∀ i k,
    ContinuousOn (fun y : ℝ × Space => D k (fun x => u y.1 x i) y.2) (Icc 0 T ×ˢ univ)
  second_continuous : ∀ T > 0, ∀ i k l,
    ContinuousOn (fun y : ℝ × Space => D l (D k (fun x => u y.1 x i)) y.2) (Icc 0 T ×ˢ univ)
  pressure_continuous : ∀ T > 0,
    ContinuousOn (fun y : ℝ × Space => p y.1 y.2) (Icc 0 T ×ˢ univ)
  pressure_gradient_continuous : ∀ T > 0, ∀ i,
    ContinuousOn (fun y : ℝ × Space => D i (p y.1) y.2) (Icc 0 T ×ˢ univ)
```

```lean
theorem main_result :
    ∃ B : ℕ, 0 < B ∧
      (∀ s : ℝ, |deriv (deriv SmoothBump.profile) s| ≤ (B:ℝ)) ∧
    ∀ (ν : ℝ), 0 < ν → ComputableReal ν →
    ∀ (b N : ℕ) [NeZero b] [NeZero N]
      (M : TM0.Machine (Fin b) (Fin N)) (w : List (Fin b)),
    ∃ (Ftor Fcyl : ℝ → VectorField 3),
      (compile B M w).Represents .torus Ftor ∧
      (compile B M w).Represents .cylinder Fcyl ∧
      (ContDiff ℝ ∞ (uncurry (Ftor ν)) ∧
        (∀ t, FactorsThrough (Ftor ν t) PeriodicSpace.cover) ∧
        (∃ K : Set (Coord 2), IsCompact K ∧ (∀ X ∈ K, ∀ i, X i ∈ Ioo (0:ℝ) 1) ∧
          ∀ t X, PeriodicSpace.cover (horizontal X) ∉ PeriodicSpace.cover '' K → Ftor ν t X = 0) ∧
        (∀ T d, ∃ C : ℝ, 0 ≤ C ∧ ∀ t ∈ Icc (0:ℝ) T, ∀ X,
          ‖iteratedFDeriv ℝ d (uncurry (Ftor ν)) (t,X)‖ ≤ C) ∧
        ∃ u : VectorField 3,
          ContDiff ℝ ∞ (uncurry u) ∧ TorusClass.ComparisonClass u (fun _ _ => 0) ∧
          NavierStokes ν u (fun _ _ => 0) (Ftor ν) ∧
          (Observation.torusEvent u ↔ (TM0.eval M w).Dom) ∧
          ∀ (v : VectorField 3) (p : ScalarField 3), TorusClass.ComparisonClass v p →
            NavierStokes ν v p (Ftor ν) →
            ∀ t ≥ 0, (∀ X, v t X = u t X) ∧ (∀ X, p t X = 0)) ∧
      (ContDiff ℝ ∞ (uncurry (Fcyl ν)) ∧
        UniformDerivatives.Bounded (fun _ : Unit => uncurry (Fcyl ν)) ∧
        (∀ t X z, Fcyl ν t (update X 2 z) = Fcyl ν t X) ∧
        (∀ T : ℝ, ∃ K : Set (Coord 2), IsCompact K ∧ ∀ t ∈ Icc (0:ℝ) T,
          ∀ X : Coord 3, horizontal X ∉ K → Fcyl ν t X = 0) ∧
        ∃ u : VectorField 3,
          ContDiff ℝ ∞ (uncurry u) ∧
          Cylinder.ComparisonClass (fun t x => u t (Cylinder.join x)) (fun _ _ => 0) ∧
          NavierStokes ν u (fun _ _ => 0) (Fcyl ν) ∧
          (∀ t ≥ 0, (∀ x, 0 ≤ u t (Cylinder.join x) 2) ∧
            Integrable (fun x => u t (Cylinder.join x) 2) Cylinder.measure) ∧
          (Observation.planeEvent u ↔ (TM0.eval M w).Dom) ∧
          ∀ (v : VectorField 3) (p : ScalarField 3),
            Cylinder.ComparisonClass (fun t x => v t (Cylinder.join x))
              (fun t x => p t (Cylinder.join x)) →
            NavierStokes ν v p (Fcyl ν) →
            ∀ t ≥ 0, (∀ X, v t X = u t X) ∧ (∀ X, p t X = 0))
```

    {
      "docstring": null,
      "kind": "structure",
      "name": "ComparisonClass",
      "statement_lean": "structure ComparisonClass (u : VectorField 3) (p : ScalarField 3) : Prop where\n  velocity_slices : ∀ t ≥ 0, ∀ i, ContDiff ℝ 2 (fun x => u t x i)\n  pressure_slices : ∀ t ≥ 0, ContDiff ℝ 1 (p t)\n  velocity_periodic : ∀ t ≥ 0, FactorsThrough (u t) PeriodicSpace.cover\n  pressure_periodic : ∀ t ≥ 0, periodic (p t)\n  pressure_mean : ∀ t ≥ 0, (∫ x, p t x ∂measure) = 0\n  time_derivative : ∀ t ≥ 0, ∀ x i,\n    HasDerivWithinAt (fun s => u s x i) (dt u t x i) (Ici 0) t\n  velocity_continuous : ∀ T > 0, ∀ i,\n    ContinuousOn (fun y : ℝ × Space => u y.1 y.2 i) (Icc 0 T ×ˢ univ)\n  time_continuous : ∀ T > 0, ∀ i,\n    ContinuousOn (fun y : ℝ × Space => dt u y.1 y.2 i) (Icc 0 T ×ˢ univ)\n  gradient_continuous : ∀ T > 0, ∀ i k,\n    ContinuousOn (fun y : ℝ × Space => D k (fun x => u y.1 x i) y.2) (Icc 0 T ×ˢ univ)\n  second_continuous : ∀ T > 0, ∀ i k l,\n    ContinuousOn (fun y : ℝ × Space => D l (D k (fun x => u y.1 x i)) y.2) (Icc 0 T ×ˢ univ)\n  pressure_continuous : ∀ T > 0,\n    ContinuousOn (fun y : ℝ × Space => p y.1 y.2) (Icc 0 T ×ˢ univ)\n  pressure_gradient_continuous : ∀ T > 0, ∀ i,\n    ContinuousOn (fun y : ℝ × Space => D i (p y.1) y.2) (Icc 0 T ×ˢ univ)"
    },

Each map acts on every point of its source sheet, and the construction provides effective bounds for all mixed derivatives.\n\nThis selected statement covers the sheet programs. The paper's fixed-particle halting detector and its reciprocal maps on solid boxes are outside its scope.\n\nThe formalization proves that a fluid particle can detect arbitrary finite-machine halting using alternating-coordinate memory in smooth incompressible Navier–Stokes flows whose velocity and force decay faster than every inverse power of time, including every mixed derivative. For each fixed positive computable viscosity, the construction starts from rest and provides effective forces, unique material trajectories, and an exact equivalence between entering a fixed open detector and halting.\n\nThe velocity and force share one compact support for every machine and input. On $\\mathbb R^2\\times\\mathbb T$, it is equivalent to the integral of the nonnegative vertical velocity over the observation half-plane times the circle exceeding $1/2$.\n\nThe torus force is confined to a fixed horizontal region. Source boxes are pairwise disjoint and target boxes are pairwise disjoint, while overlap between the two families is allowed. Each map holds on a neighborhood of the entire source box, and the velocity is supported in the middle half of the time interval.\n\nIt also encodes every finite machine and input in a smooth, compactly supported incompressible Navier–Stokes flow on $\\mathbb R^3$ starting from rest: the particle initially at $(4,0,0)$ enters the fixed open box $(-1,2)^3$ exactly when the machine halts. One velocity works for every viscosity $\\nu>0$, with zero pressure and force $f_0+\\nu f_1$. Velocity and force have bounded mixed derivatives and are one-periodic after time one; the repeated velocity depends only on the machine. The force is computable for computable $\\nu$. <a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


We construct smooth forces for three-dimensional incompressible Navier–Stokes flow, from rest at any fixed positive computable viscosity, whose velocity field detects whether a prescribed machine halts. On the unit flat torus, a pointwise test of the third velocity component uses successively shorter stirring intervals in a fixed spatial region. On $`\mathbb R^2\times\mathbb T`$, an integral test uses an expanding array of translation regions and a force with globally bounded mixed derivatives. The vertical velocity solves an advection–diffusion equation: short bursts control its pointwise error in the first construction, and a moving cutoff controls the total escaped mass in the second.

<a id="pm-h-1"></a>
#### Statements index

| anchor | kind | label | number | first sentence |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| [stmt-thm-main](#stmt-thm-main) | theorem | `thm:main` | 1 | For each positive computable $\nu$, a machine and a finite input effectively determine each of the following two forces.* |
| [stmt-thm-compact-detector](#stmt-thm-compact-detector) | theorem | `thm:compact-detector` | 2 | For $V,p,E$ as above, choose either of the two pairs |
| [stmt-lem-torus-scalar](#stmt-lem-torus-scalar) | lemma | `lem:torus-scalar` | 3 | For smooth $a,h$ on $[0,T]\times\mathbb T^2$ with $\mathop{\mathrm{div}}_Ya=0$, smooth initial data give a unique smooth solution of $w_t+a\cdot\nabla_Yw=\Delta_Yw+h$. |
| [stmt-lem-heat-bounds](#stmt-lem-heat-bounds) | lemma | `lem:heat-bounds` | 4 | For either $(d,H)$ in [eq:two-constants], the unit two-torus heat kernel satisfies |
| [stmt-cbe-scalar-lemma](#stmt-cbe-scalar-lemma) | lemma | `cbe:scalar-lemma` | 5 | Equation [cbe:scalar] has a global smooth solution. |
| [stmt-auto-corollary-1](#stmt-auto-corollary-1) | corollary | - | 6 | There is no algorithm deciding either fixed velocity event in Theorem 1 for every force description produced by its respective construction.* |

<a id="sec:introduction"></a>
### Observing the velocity rather than a particle

A material observer follows one marked fluid particle. An Eulerian observer instead examines the velocity at a time, without remembering which particle reached which point. The distinction matters for computation. A smooth flow can transport an exact tape encoding, but a velocity component that carries the same information is also diffused by viscosity. We ask whether a fixed test of the velocity can still detect halting when the fluid starts at rest.

<a id="eq:NS"></a>
Write $\mathbb T=\mathbb R/\mathbb Z$. On either $\Omega=\mathbb T^3$ or $\Omega=\mathbb R^2\times\mathbb T$, we use the flat metric and the equations $$\label{eq:NS}
 \partial_tu+(u\cdot\nabla)u=-\nabla p+\nu\Delta u+f,
 \qquad \mathop{\mathrm{div}}u=0,\qquad u(0)=0.$$ The viscosity $\nu>0$ is fixed and computable. The input consists of a deterministic Turing machine and a finite input word. An effective force prescription is a finite program evaluating $f$ and each requested mixed derivative, to any rational error, at computably supplied space-time arguments. It also gives the finite derivative bounds used in the construction. There is no computational complexity requirement.

<a id="eq:torus-event"></a>
<a id="eq:plane-event"></a>
<a id="eq:comparison-class"></a>
We use two fixed observations. On $\mathbb T^3$, with coordinates $(x,y,z)$, the event is $$\label{eq:torus-event}
 \exists t\ge0\ \exists (x,y,z),\quad
 1/32<y<1/8,\qquad u_3(t,x,y,z)>\tfrac12.$$ On $\mathbb R^2\times\mathbb T$, with coordinates $(X_1,X_2,z)$, it is $$\label{eq:plane-event}
 \exists t\ge0,\qquad
 \int_{\{X_2>0\}\times\mathbb T}u_3(t,X,z)\,\mathrm dX\,\mathrm dz>\tfrac12.$$ All integrals over a torus coordinate use one period and normalized Lebesgue measure. We specify the solution classes before the theorem. A classical competitor has continuous velocity, time derivative, spatial derivatives through order two, pressure and pressure gradient on every finite closed time cylinder, and satisfies the equations pointwise. On the torus its pressure is periodic and has mean zero. On $\mathbb R^2\times\mathbb T$ they additionally satisfy, for every finite $T$, $$\label{eq:comparison-class}
 \begin{gathered}
 u\in C([0,T];H^2)\cap C^1([0,T];L^2),\qquad
 p\in C([0,T];H^1),\\
 \sup_{[0,T]\times\Omega}(|u|+|\nabla u|)<\infty.
 \end{gathered}$$ The pressure condition on the noncompact domain fixes the additive spatial constant. Smoothness at time zero means one-sided smoothness.

<a id="stmt-thm-main"></a>
<div id="thm:main" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1**. *For each positive computable $\nu$, a machine and a finite input effectively determine each of the following two forces.*

1.  *On $\mathbb T^3$, a smooth force has a unique global smooth solution in the torus class above, with pressure zero, for which <a href="#eq:torus-event" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:torus-event">[eq:torus-event]</a> holds exactly when the machine halts. The force is supported in $K\times\mathbb T$ for a compact $K\subset(0,1)^2$ independent of time. Its derivatives are bounded on every finite time interval; their bounds may grow with that interval.*

2.  *On $\mathbb R^2\times\mathbb T$, a smooth force and all its mixed derivatives are globally bounded. On each finite time interval its horizontal support is compact. It has a unique global smooth solution in <a href="#eq:comparison-class" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:comparison-class">[eq:comparison-class]</a>, with pressure zero and integrable nonnegative third component. Event <a href="#eq:plane-event" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:plane-event">[eq:plane-event]</a> holds exactly when the machine halts.*

*The sets and thresholds in both observations are independent of the machine and input. The compact set $K$ in the first construction may depend on that instance, but is independent of time. Both force prescriptions install all the required local instructions, without executing the selected computation.*

</div>

The two conclusions pay for diffusion in different ways. The first keeps the horizontal mechanism inside one chart and speeds up its later tests. Its force need not be bounded uniformly for infinite time. The second keeps the amplitudes and all mixed derivatives bounded by allowing the horizontal geometry to expand. It makes no claim of a single compact support for all time or of uniformly bounded total kinetic energy. Neither construction requires a solenoidal force or a mean-zero force. These qualifications are part of the mathematical distinction between the regimes.

The descriptions specify exact computable forces through arbitrarily accurate evaluations. The strict observation margins proved below concern these forces; no stability under perturbations of the force or finite-precision replacement of its description is asserted.

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
#### Background and the two mechanisms

Turing proved the undecidability of whether a described machine ever prints a designated symbol (Turing 1936--1937, sec. 8) <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[Turing1936](#ref-Turing1936)]. Replacing that printing event by a halt gives the decision problem used here. Moore’s generalized shifts encode tapes in positional real coordinates and represent local instructions by piecewise affine maps (Moore 1990, 1991) <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[Moore1990](#ref-Moore1990), [Moore1991](#ref-Moore1991)]. Smooth flows require injective finite-time maps, whereas a machine may erase a symbol. Landauer (Landauer 1961, sec. 3) <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[Landauer1961](#ref-Landauer1961)] discusses retaining information that an irreversible operation discards; Bennett (Bennett 1973) <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[Bennett1973](#ref-Bennett1973)] gives a reversible simulation that records the instruction history. The particular one-head compiler and planar realization used here are supplied by (OpenAI 2026c, Lemma 3.1 and Theorem 4.1) <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[stationary2026](#ref-stationary2026)]; the diffusion estimates below are proved here.

Material-particle computation was realized by Cardona, Miranda, Peralta-Salas and Presas in steady Euler flow on a three-sphere with a chosen Riemannian metric (Cardona et al. 2021, Theorem 6.1) <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[CardonaEtAl2021](#ref-CardonaEtAl2021)]. Dyhr, González-Prieto, Miranda and Peralta-Salas construct steady unforced Navier–Stokes flows on suitable Riemannian three-manifolds, using harmonic fields for the Hodge Laplacian and an adapted metric (Dyhr et al. 2026, Theorem A) <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[DyhrEtAl2026](#ref-DyhrEtAl2026)]. These results concern trajectories of a stationary velocity, whereas the observations here test the evolving velocity itself.

Velocity-space observation has a direct predecessor in the work of Cardona, Miranda and Peralta-Salas (Cardona, Miranda, and Peralta-Salas 2022) <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[CardonaMirandaPeraltaSalas2022](#ref-CardonaMirandaPeraltaSalas2022)]. Their construction uses the unforced incompressible Euler evolution on a constructed high-dimensional compact Riemannian manifold; computation is observed by entry into an open set of divergence-free velocity fields. Here the geometry is flat and three-dimensional, the viscosity is positive, and the initial velocity is zero. The machine and input enter a prescribed external force, and the observations are the pointwise and integral tests above. These differences distinguish the present problem from both stationary-particle and Euler velocity-space computation.

The common analytic device is particularly simple. For a planar divergence-free field $a(t,Y)$ and a scalar $w(t,Y)$, the three-dimensional velocity $(a,w)$ is divergence free when it is independent of $z$. Its third equation is an advection–diffusion equation. We prescribe its source and the horizontal force, and then solve for $w$. Thus the unknown velocity is not concealed in a force formula.

In the torus construction, each block injects a very small bump of height one at the initial machine code and runs a longer prefix of the planar processor. A short physical duration keeps diffusion close to pure transport, while a long heat-only interval makes older bumps small. A halting run carries a fresh bump to the detector. In a nonhalting run, the entire transported bump remains separated from it, so even its diffusive tail stays below the threshold.

For bounded forcing, an initial impulse creates scalar mass one. The horizontal field translates large squares between integer addresses for all possible configurations. Longer and larger gates keep every force derivative bounded. A cutoff moving with the chosen address cancels transport exactly; its Laplacian measures the diffusion loss. Choosing the gate radii fast enough makes the sum of those losses less than $1/24$.

Section <a href="#sec:scalar-structure" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:scalar-structure">2</a> establishes the scalar reduction and the solution convention. Section <a href="#sec:compact" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:compact">3</a> proves a parameterized injection, stirring and waiting theorem, including two quantitative specializations. Section <a href="#sec:compact-application" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:compact-application">4</a> applies it to the fixed torus detector. The expanding-address construction and its mass estimate appear in Section <a href="#sec:bounded" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:bounded">5</a>. The classical background for the maximum principle and parabolic kernels is described, for example, by Aronson (Aronson 1968) <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[Aronson1968](#ref-Aronson1968)]; all constants and comparison arguments needed for detection are included below.

<a id="sec:scalar-structure"></a>
### A scalar component of a viscous velocity

<a id="eq:triangular-force"></a>
<a id="eq:scalar"></a>
Let $Y$ range over $\mathbb T^2$ or $\mathbb R^2$. Suppose $a(t,Y)$ is divergence free, and prescribe the horizontal residual and the vertical source by $$\label{eq:triangular-force}
 F=\partial_ta+(a\cdot\nabla_Y)a-\nu\Delta_Ya,
 \qquad f(t,Y,z)=(F(t,Y),h(t,Y)).$$ If $$\label{eq:scalar}
 \partial_tw+a\cdot\nabla_Yw=\nu\Delta_Yw+h,
 \qquad w(0)=0,$$ then $U=(a,w)$, $p=0$, satisfies <a href="#eq:NS" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:NS">[eq:NS]</a>, provided $a(0)=0$. Indeed $\mathop{\mathrm{div}}U=\mathop{\mathrm{div}}_Ya$, all $z$ derivatives vanish, and the horizontal and vertical equations are exactly <a href="#eq:triangular-force" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:triangular-force">[eq:triangular-force]</a> and <a href="#eq:scalar" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:scalar">[eq:scalar]</a>. Only $a$ and $h$ occur in the force prescription.

<a id="eq:uniqueness"></a>
We will construct the scalar solution in each setting, including its behavior at infinity in the noncompact case. Once it belongs to the stated class, uniqueness is the classical difference-energy argument (Leray 1934, sec. 18) <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[Leray1934](#ref-Leray1934)], in the precise form proved in (OpenAI 2026a, Lemma 3) <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[entry2026](#ref-entry2026)]. To display its use, let $v$ be a competitor, $e=v-U$, and $q$ the pressure difference. Then $$\partial_te+(v\cdot\nabla)e+(e\cdot\nabla)U
       =-\nabla q+\nu\Delta e,$$ and $$\label{eq:uniqueness}
 \frac12\frac{d}{dt}\|e\|_2^2+\nu\|\nabla e\|_2^2
       \le\|\nabla U\|_{\infty,\mathrm{op}}\|e\|_2^2.$$ Periodic integration proves this on the torus. On $\mathbb R^2\times\mathbb T$, insert a horizontal cutoff with gradient $O(R^{-1})$. The transport, pressure and viscous boundary errors are bounded respectively by $$C R^{-1}\|v\|_\infty\|e\|_2^2,\quad
 C R^{-1}\|q\|_2\|e\|_2,\quad
 C R^{-1}\|\nabla e\|_2\|e\|_2.$$ All terms converge by <a href="#eq:comparison-class" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:comparison-class">[eq:comparison-class]</a>; its time regularity justifies differentiating the energy. Let $R\to\infty$ and apply Gronwall to <a href="#eq:uniqueness" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:uniqueness">[eq:uniqueness]</a> on $[0,T]$. Since $e(0)=0$, the velocities agree, and the pressure normalization fixes $q=0$. This comparison gives uniqueness for the explicitly constructed data, not existence for arbitrary three-dimensional data.

<a id="sec:compact"></a>
### Short stirring bursts on the torus

This section separates the diffusion argument from the construction of a planar machine. The input is a smooth periodic planar field and a distinguished initial point. Its orbit either enters a target at an integer phase or stays a positive distance away at every phase. The output is a force for which the third velocity component distinguishes these two cases.

<a id="eq:processor-derivatives"></a>
We first work at viscosity one. Let $V(s,Y)$ be an effectively specified smooth divergence-free field on $\mathbb R\times\mathbb T^2$, periodic of period one and zero on a neighborhood of every integer phase. Its flow from phase zero to phase $s$ is denoted $\Psi_s$. Assume that effective uniform derivative bounds are available; choose an integer $L\ge1$ such that $$\label{eq:processor-derivatives}
 \|D_YV\|_{\infty,\mathrm{op}},\quad
 \|D_Y^2V\|_{\infty,\mathrm{op}}\le L,
 \qquad R=4^L.$$ All derivative norms in this section are Euclidean operator norms. Let $p\in\mathbb T^2$ be computable and let $E\subset\mathbb T^2$ be an open target.

<a id="stmt-thm-compact-detector"></a>
<div id="thm:compact-detector" class="theorem">

<a id="eq:two-constants"></a>
**Theorem 2** (Injection, stirring and waiting). *For $V,p,E$ as above, choose either of the two pairs $$\label{eq:two-constants}
 (d,H)=(1/32,10^9)\quad\hbox{or}\quad(1/16,10^8).$$ There is a smooth force on $\mathbb T^3$, effectively determined by $V,p$, for which zero initial velocity has a global smooth solution, unique in the classical torus class of Section <a href="#sec:introduction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:introduction">1</a>, with pressure zero and the following properties.*

1.  *If $\Psi_k(p)\in E$ for some integer $k\ge0$, then $u_3(t,Y,z)>1/2$ for some finite $t$ and some $Y\in E$.*

2.  *If $\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}_{\mathbb T^2}(\Psi_s(p),E)\ge2d$ for every $s\ge0$, then $u_3(t,Y,z)<1/2$ for every $t\ge0$ and $Y\in E$.*

*The third component is nonnegative. At viscosity one, its total horizontal mass is less than $10^{-11}$. The same conclusions and threshold hold for every positive computable viscosity, with the time and horizontal velocity rescaled as in <a href="#eq:compact-viscosity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:compact-viscosity">[eq:compact-viscosity]</a>. The force is supported in the union of the horizontal support of $V$ and one fixed small neighborhood of $p$, times $\mathbb T$. All smoothness and uniqueness assertions concern every finite time interval.*

</div>

The force does not depend on $E$, so no effective presentation of the open target is required. The target enters only the two orbit tests. Here $d$ is the clearance required between a transported bump and the target, and $H$ is an upper bound for the heat kernel at that clearance or after one unit of waiting. The proof establishes that bound in Lemma <a href="#lem:heat-bounds" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:heat-bounds">4</a>. The nonhalting hypothesis concerns the entire orbit. Integer-time separation alone would leave open a false detection during a transition. The first numerical pair serves the fixed torus detector in Section <a href="#sec:compact-application" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:compact-application">4</a>; the second serves the slow-clock application identified at the end of that section. The scalar proof is the same for both.

<a id="sec:compact-blocks"></a>
#### An injection and a finite computation in each block

<a id="eq:compact-scales"></a>
<a id="eq:bump-bounds"></a>
Use the effective nondecreasing smooth step $$b(r)=\begin{cases}e^{-1/r},&r>0,\\0,&r\le0,\end{cases}
 \qquad \theta(r)=\frac{b(r)}{b(r)+b(1-r)}.$$ It is zero for $r\le0$ and one for $r\ge1$, with all derivatives flat at the endpoints. Define a fixed bump on $\mathbb R^2$ by $$g(Z)=\prod_{j=1}^2\theta(2(Z_j+1))\theta(2(1-Z_j)).$$ It lies in $[0,1]$, equals one near zero, and is supported in $[-1,1]^2$. Let an effective integer $C\ge1$ bound its first and second derivative norms. For $n\ge1$, put $$\label{eq:compact-scales}
 \begin{aligned}
 b_n&=10^{-6}(2R)^{-n},&g_n(Y)&=g((Y-p)/b_n),\\
 D_n&=2Cb_n^{-2}(1+nL)R^{3n},&
 \delta_n&=\frac1{100(1+D_n)},\qquad t_n=2n.
 \end{aligned}$$ The width $b_n$ makes the bump’s mass small and its transported support narrow. The quantity $D_n$ is chosen to bound the Laplacian of that transported bump; Section <a href="#sec:compact-error" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:compact-error">3.3</a> verifies the bound. The duration $\delta_n$ then makes the accumulated diffusion error at most $2\delta_nD_n<1/16$. These are separate roles: spatial width controls the mass and separation, while physical duration controls the error in height. Here $g_n$ is periodized from the small square about a lift of $p$; the copies have disjoint supports. In particular $$\label{eq:bump-bounds}
 \|\nabla g_n\|_\infty\le Cb_n^{-1},\qquad
 \|D^2g_n\|_\infty\le Cb_n^{-2},\qquad
 \int_{\mathbb T^2}g_n\le4b_n^2.$$

Block $n$ injects this bump over time $\delta_n$, runs $n$ complete periods of $V$ over another time $\delta_n$, and then waits for the next block. In particular, its heat-only wait has length $2-2\delta_n>1$. The time diagram in Figure <a href="#fig:blocks" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:blocks">1</a> shows the roles of these intervals; their plotted lengths are schematic.

<figure id="fig:blocks">

<figcaption>One torus block. Fresh scalar is injected at the initial code, then transported through a longer finite prefix. The wait exceeds one unit of heat time. The two active intervals are much shorter than the wait; their relative sizes in the diagram are not to scale.</figcaption>
</figure>
